ds_{q}^{2}{}_{\text{around bdy}}=d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2}+\delta_{ij}dz^{i}dz^{j};\quad\rho\in[0,\infty),\psi\in[0,2\pi q),

(7.37)

where
x-x_{1}=\rho\cos(\psi)
and
\tau-\tau_{0}=\rho\sin(\psi)
. With a change of coordinates
\rho=qr^{q}
and
\psi=q\theta
we can write the metric in the form

ds_{q}^{2}{}_{\text{around bdy}}=e^{-4V(r)}(dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2})+\delta_{ij}dz^{i}dz^{j};\quad r\in[0,\infty),\psi\in[0,2\pi),

(7.38)

where
V(r)=2\lambda\log(r)
and
\lambda=(q-1)/4
. The advantage of doing this is that the
q
dependence is now only in
\lambda
and not in the range of any of the coordinates.

The generalization to replica metric with multiple (say
N
) conical singularities (one at each
\{x_{i},\tau_{i}\}
,
i\in\{1,2,...,N\}
), corresponding to
M_{1}
with flat metric (
3.1
) is given by,

ds_{q}^{2}{}_{\text{around bdy}}=e^{-4\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{i}(r_{i})}(dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2})+\delta_{ij}dz^{i}dz^{j};\quad r\in[0,\infty),\psi\in[0,2\pi),

(7.39)

where
V_{i}=2\lambda_{i}\log(r_{i})
,
r_{i}=\sqrt{(x-x_{i})^{2}+(\tau-\tau_{i})^{2}}
is the distance from each conical sigularity,
\lambda_{i}=(q_{i}-1)/4
. In our case
q_{i}=q
and
\tau_{i}=\tau_{0}

\forall\,i
.

For a CFT on
M_{1}
we can now use the replica conservation and trace equations
\nabla_{\mu}^{(q)}\langle T^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\rangle_{q}=0
,
T^{\mu}_{\ \mu}=\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]
. The homogenous solution in
\{r,\tau,y^{i}\}
basis is given by

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{\ \nu}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=C\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{i}(q)}{r_{i}^{4}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-\frac{d(\tau-\tau_{i})^{2}}{r_{i}^{2}}&\frac{d(x-x_{i})(\tau-\tau_{i})}{r_{i}^{2}}\\
\frac{d(x-x_{i})(\tau-\tau_{i})}{r_{i}^{2}}&(1-d)+\frac{d(\tau-\tau_{i})^{2}}{r_{i}^{2}}\end{array}\right);\quad\{\mu,\nu\}\in\{r,\tau\},

\displaystyle=C\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{i}(q)}{r_{i}^{4}}\delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu};\quad\{\mu,\nu\}\in\{i,j\},

(7.42)

where
C
is a constant. In the case of
N
conical singularities the net contribution is the sum of contribution from each conical singularity.

Replica stress tensor vev:

We will now consider the replica stress tensor vev in the case of two disconnected planar entangling boundaries on flat space. In the case of an entangling region with disconnected boundary
x=x_{1}
and
x=x_{2}
spanning
z^{i}{}^{\prime}s
on a constant time slice, we have two conical singularities at the two disconnected boundaries. We also have an additional dimensionful parameter
L=x_{2}-x_{1}
on which the
\langle T^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\rangle_{q}
can depend on. Since the boundaries
\Sigma
has no extrinsic curvature, we still have
\partial_{\psi}
and
\partial_{i}
as killing vectors on
M_{1}
and
M_{q}
. We will now solve (
7.7
) taking these into account. The
i-
conservation equation implies

\langle T^{i}_{\ i}\rangle_{q}=\tilde{g}_{1}(\rho,a,L)=(d-2)\left(\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)+\tilde{g}(\rho,a,L)\right),

(7.43)

where
C_{d}(q)
is a
O(q-1)
function of
q
, and
K(\rho,L)
is a dimensionless function capturing the
L
dependence in the regulator independent part. Using (
7.8
),we get

\displaystyle\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\partial_{\rho}(\rho(\mathcal{A}^{(q)}-\langle T^{i}_{\ i}\rangle_{q}))

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\rho^{2}}\left(\frac{(d-2)C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d-2}}K(\rho,L)-\int d\rho(d-2)\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d-1}}K(\rho,L)\right)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\partial_{\rho}(\rho\tilde{f}(\rho,a))

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-d)C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d-2}}+\tilde{f}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\tilde{f},

(7.44)

where
\tilde{f}(\rho,a)=\mathcal{A}^{(q)}-(d-2)\tilde{g}(\rho,a)
.
The trace equation implies,

\displaystyle\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}_{q}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{(q)}-\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle_{q}-\langle T^{i}_{\ i}\rangle_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d-2}}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\tilde{f}.

(7.45)

The function
K(\rho,L)
must satisfy the following properties,

1.

It is dimensionless since
\langle T^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\rangle_{q}
has mass dimension
d
which implies
K(\rho,L)
can only depend on the ratio
\rho/L
.

2.

For
\rho>>L
,
K(\rho/L)\to 1
, since far away from the entangling region it must be indistinguishable from a single conical singularity.

Both these conditions imply
K(\rho,L)
must be of the form,

K(\rho,L)=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{i}\left(\frac{L}{\rho}\right)^{i};\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

(7.46)

We have,

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{\ r}\rangle_{q}\rvert_{a=0}

\displaystyle=\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle_{q}\cos^{2}(\psi)+\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle_{q}\sin^{2}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)[1-d\sin^{2}(\psi)]-\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}(q)}{\rho^{d-2}}.

(7.47)

8
Entanglement entropy - vacuum states

We will now calculate the scaling of the Rényi and EE for a vacuum state on a
d
dimensional background, and an entangling boundary with no extrinsic curvature. We make use of the results for the replica stress tensor vev from the previous section. Using (
5.51
), the scaling of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
in this case is given by,

-\alpha\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim}}}{\partial r_{1}}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g_{rr}\langle T^{rr}\rangle^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\tau\tau}\langle T^{\tau\tau}\rangle^{[1]}\right),

(8.1)

where we have kept
r_{2}
fixed and scaled
r_{1}
. We can instead scale
r_{2}
and keep
r_{1}
fixed simply by interchanging
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
in the expression above. We recover the scaling of the EE in the limit
q\to 1
. Notice also that
\delta_{\Delta r}g_{r\tau}=\delta_{\Delta r}g_{ij}=0
in static backgrounds with zero extrinsic curvature for
\Sigma
.

On a flat background metric, we have translation symmetry along
r
or
x
, therefore
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
only depends on
\Delta r
or
\Delta x
. Therefore it is enough to consider the scaling of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
wrt scaling the entire interval
\Delta r\to e^{\alpha}\Delta r
or
\Delta x\to e^{\alpha}\Delta x
, as opposed to keeping one of the boundaries fixed and scaling the other. On scaling the metric, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\alpha g_{rr};\quad\mu=\nu=r,

(8.2)

\displaystyle=0;\quad\text{otherwise}.

(8.3)

In flat space vacuum CFT state we have
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[0]}=0
, therefore

\langle T^{\mu}_{\hskip 5.69046pt\nu}\rangle^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[1]}\langle T_{\alpha\nu}\rangle^{[0]}+g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}\langle T_{\alpha\nu}\rangle^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}\langle T_{\alpha\nu}\rangle^{[1]},

(8.4)

i.e., for the vacuum state we can raise and lower indices in
\langle T^{\mu}_{\hskip 5.69046pt\nu}\rangle^{[1]}
simply by using the metric on the base manifold
M_{1}
,
therefore,

\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle^{[1]}.

(8.5)

Notice that the scaling is now with respect to
\Delta r
. This is because in flat space
\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\mu\nu}
is independent of whether we scale wrt
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
or
r_{2}-r_{1}=\Delta r
. This reflects the translational symmetry along
r
in flat space.

As seen in the previous section, the replica stress tensor has regulator (
a
) dependent terms and regulator-independent terms, which needs to be treated appropriately when performing replica integrals (
6.2
). We will separately calculate the contributions from both kind of terms. The complete result for scaling of Rényi and EE is the sum of both contributions.

8.1
Regulator independent contribution

Let us now consider only the regulator (
a
) independent contribution to the replica stress tensor vev. These are non local and contribute over the entire entangling region.

Approach 1 - As the sum of individual conical stress tensor:

We first consider the regulator (
a
) independent contribution to the replica stress tensor vev given by (
7.20
). In
\{r,\tau,y^{i}\}
basis we have

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}=

\displaystyle\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}\cos^{2}(\psi)+\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}\sin^{2}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{d}}\left[C_{d}^{[1]}+\frac{Q_{d}^{[1]}}{1-d}\right][1-d\sin^{2}(\psi)]+\frac{m^{2}}{\rho^{(d-2)}}(M_{d}^{[1]}+N_{d}^{[1]})\left[\frac{1+(2-d)\sin^{2}(\psi)}{(3-d)}\right],

\displaystyle\langle T^{\tau}_{\tau}\rangle^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}=

\displaystyle\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}\sin^{2}(\psi)+\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}\cos^{2}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{d}}\left[C_{d}^{[1]}+\frac{Q_{d}^{[1]}}{1-d}\right][(1-d)+d\sin^{2}(\psi)]+\frac{m^{2}}{\rho^{(d-2)}}(M_{d}^{[1]}+N_{d}^{[1]})\left[1+\frac{(d-2)}{(3-d)}\sin^{2}(\psi)\right],

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{\tau}\rangle^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}=

\displaystyle\langle T_{r}^{\tau}\rangle^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}=\left(\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}-\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}^{[1]}_{\text{$a$=0}}\right)\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{\rho^{d}}\left[C_{d}^{[1]}+\frac{Q_{d}^{[1]}}{1-d}\right]+\frac{(d-2)m^{2}}{(3-d)\rho^{(d-2)}}\left(M_{d}^{[1]}+N_{d}^{[1]}\right)\right)\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi),

\displaystyle\langle T^{i}_{j}\rangle^{[1]}_{a=0}=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{i}_{j}}{\rho^{d}}\left(C_{d}^{[1]}+R_{d}^{[1]}+(m\rho)^{2}\left[\frac{(d-4)M_{d}^{[1]}}{(3-d)(d-2)}+S_{d}^{[1]}\right]\right).

(8.6)

We have
C_{d}(q)
given by (
7.31
). From this we see that
C_{d}^{[1]}=\frac{-(n_{s}+6n_{f}+12n_{v})}{360\pi^{2}}
. We can read off the other undetermined coefficients depending on
q
by comparing our result (
7.20
) with the result in literature obtained by directly computing on conical manifolds for scalar case, given by (
7.27
).

In the case of a disconnected boundary (at
r=r_{1}
and
r=r_{2}
) we have two conical singularity, one at each boundary. As we have just seen in the case of multiple conical singularities, the net contribution to the
O(q-1)
replica stress tensor is the sum of contribution from each conical singularity. We perform the integral restricted around the entangling region. In
\{r,\tau\}
plane these are semi discs of radius
(r_{2}-r_{1})/2
, with
r\geq r_{1}
around
r_{1}
and
r\leq r_{2}
around
r_{2}
. Since the
O(q-1)
replica stress tensor has identical contributions from each conical singularity and the net contribution is the sum of individual conical contribution, this integral is the same as integrating over a disc of radius
(r_{2}-r_{1})/2
around one of the conical singularities.

Figure 8
:
Region of integration

Using (
8.5
), the non local contribution to the entanglement entropy
S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}
(i.e, contribution depending on the entire entangling region and not just the entangling boundary) in
d-
dimensional flat space with an entangling region of size
\Delta r
with a zero extrinsic curvature boundary is

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg indep}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{\Sigma}d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\int_{0}^{2\pi q}d\psi\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\rho\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle_{a=0}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\left(\frac{A_{d}}{\rho^{d-1}}+\frac{m^{2}B_{d}}{\rho^{d-3}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi A_{2}\log\left(\frac{\Delta r}{2\epsilon}\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}m^{2}B_{2}(\Delta r)^{2};\quad d=2,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\left\{A_{4}\left(-\frac{2}{(\Delta r)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}\right)+m^{2}B_{4}\log\left(\frac{\Delta r}{2\epsilon}\right)\right\};\quad d=4,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\left\{\frac{A_{d}}{2-d}\left(\frac{2^{d-2}}{(\Delta r)^{d-2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d-2}}\right)+\frac{m^{2}B_{d}}{4-d}\left(\frac{2^{d-4}}{(\Delta r)^{d-4}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d-4}}\right)\right\};

\displaystyle\text{otherwise},

(8.7)

where
\Delta r=r_{2}-r_{1}
and
Vol(M_{d-2})=\int_{\Sigma}d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}
. In 3 dimensional flat space with
\Sigma
spanning a finite length
2\pi\phi_{0}
as in (
7.1
),
Vol(M_{d-2})=2\pi\phi_{0}
.

We have,

A_{d}=\frac{(2-d)}{2}\left(C_{d}^{[1]}+\frac{Q_{d}^{[1]}}{(1-d)}\right),\quad B_{d}=\frac{(d-4)(M_{d}^{[1]}+N_{d}^{[1]})}{2(d-3)}.

(8.8)

In
d=4
, we can read off the specific values of the coefficients from the cosmic string literature (
7.1
). For the scalar case from (
7.27
) we have we have

C_{4}^{[1]}=\frac{-1}{360\pi^{2}},\quad Q_{4}^{[1]}=\frac{1}{24\pi^{2}}(1-6\xi),\quad M_{4}^{[1]}=\frac{-1}{48\pi^{2}}(1-6\xi),\quad N_{4}^{[1]}=0.

(8.9)

For photon from (
7.32
)

C_{4}^{[1]}=\frac{-1}{30\pi^{2}},\quad Q_{4}^{[1]}=M_{4}^{[1]}=N_{4}^{[1]}=0.

(8.10)

For graviton from (
7.33
)

Q_{4}^{[1]}=\frac{-7}{5\pi^{2}},\quad C_{4}^{[1]}=M_{4}^{[1]}=N_{4}^{[1]}=0.

(8.11)

Approach 2 - Using replica stress tensor with explicit
L
dependence:

Using the result for the replica stress tensor vev in the case of disconnected planar boundaries, given by (
7.47
), the regulator dependent contribution to the scaling of the Entanglement entropy is given by,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg indep}}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{\Sigma}d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\int_{0}^{2\pi q}d\psi\int_{\epsilon}^{\alpha L}d\rho\rho\Bigg(\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)[1-d\sin^{2}(\psi)]

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt-\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=(2-d)\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\int_{\epsilon}^{\alpha L}d\rho\rho\left(\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}\left[K(\rho,L)=\sum_{i}A_{i}\left(\frac{L}{\rho}\right)^{i}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=(2-d)\pi Vol(M_{d-2})C_{d}^{[1]}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left(\frac{1}{L^{d-2}}\frac{A_{i}}{\alpha^{d-2+i}(2-d-i)}-\frac{A_{i}L^{i}}{\epsilon^{d-2+i}(2-d-i)}\right);\,i\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle=(2-d)\pi Vol(M_{d-2})C_{d}^{[1]}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left(\frac{1}{L^{d-2}}\frac{B_{i}}{\alpha^{d-2+i}}-\frac{B_{i}}{\epsilon^{d-2}}\frac{L^{i}}{\epsilon^{i}}\right);\quad i\in\mathbb{Z},

(8.12)

where
B_{i}=A_{i}/(2-d-i)
. Notice that the scaling is now with respect to
\Delta r
. This is because in flat space
\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\mu\nu}
is independent of whether we scale wrt
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
or
r_{2}-r_{1}=\Delta r
. This reflects the translational symmetry along
r
in flat space. We have the finite part of EE given by,

\displaystyle S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}\Big\rvert_{\text{reg $a$ indep, finite}}=\frac{\pi Vol(M_{d-2})C_{d}^{[1]}}{L^{d-2}}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left(\frac{B_{i}}{\alpha^{d-2+i}}\right).

(8.13)

By comparing the coefficient of the finite part with the expression we have in
[
45
]
, we find that

\pi C_{d}^{[1]}\sum_{i\geq 0}\frac{B_{i}}{\alpha^{d-2+i}}=2^{d-3}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\frac{l^{d-1}}{(2-d)G}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{d-2}{2(d-1)};\frac{d}{2(d-1)};1\right)\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2(d-1)}\right)}\right)^{2-d},

(8.14)

where the ratio
l^{d-1}/G
is related to the central charge
c
in the case of even
d
and to the number of degrees of freedom in the case of odd
d
.

Approach 3:

When there are two disconnected boundaries, the net contribution to the replica stress tensor is the sum of contribution from each boundary with a conical singularity. In general the coordinates are well defined in only a small local patch around each conical singularity and we need multiple charts to cover the entire manifold in the case of two conical singularities. If
\{\rho_{1},\psi_{1},y^{i}\}
and
\{\rho_{2},\psi_{2},y^{i}\}
are the two local coordinates around each boundary conical singularity, we have

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{\,r}\rangle{}_{a=0}=C_{d}(q)\left(\frac{1}{\rho_{1}^{d}}(1-d\sin^{2}\psi_{1})+\frac{1}{\rho_{2}^{d}}(1-d\sin^{2}\psi_{2})\right).

(8.15)

We will now consider a new set of coordinates
\{\rho,\psi\}
with
\rho=0
at
r=(r_{1}+r_{2})/2
and
\psi=0
along the axis running from
r_{1}
to
r_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{1}=\sqrt{\frac{L^{2}}{4}+L\rho\cos(\psi)+\rho^{2}},

\displaystyle\rho_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{L^{2}}{4}-L\rho\cos(\psi)+\rho^{2}},

\displaystyle\psi_{1}=\pm\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{L+2\rho\cos(\psi)}{2\sqrt{\frac{L^{2}}{4}+L\rho\cos(\psi)+\rho^{2}}}\right)+2\pi q,

\displaystyle\psi_{2}=\pm\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{2\rho\cos(\psi)-L}{2\sqrt{\frac{L^{2}}{4}-L\rho\cos(\psi)+\rho^{2}}}\right)+2\pi q;\quad q\in\mathbb{Z}.

(8.16)

We can now calculate the EE with the integral restricted to the entangling region
\rho\in[0,\frac{L=(r_{2}-r_{1})}{2}]
and
\psi\in[0,2\pi q)
. This again gives

\displaystyle S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}\Big\rvert_{\text{reg $a$ indep, finite}}\propto\frac{1}{L^{d-2}}.

(8.17)

Figure 9
:
Region of integration

For the regulator-independent replica stress tensor, when there are two disconnected boundaries, it is not very clear what the right region of integration is, although all the approaches we have considered give the same
\Delta r
or
L
dependence. However, for the regulator-dependent replica stress tensor parts, the replica corrections vanish beyond the radius set by the regulator and their integral only picks up the local contribution around each boundary.

8.2
Regulator dependent contribution

Let us now consider only the regulator (
a
) dependent contribution to the replica stress tensor vev. The
O(q-1)
terms in these, contribute locally around each conical singularity to the replica integrals. These are given by the regulator
a
dependent parts of the solutions (
7.11
), (
7.14
), (
7.15
),

\displaystyle\langle T^{i}_{\ j}\rangle-\langle T^{i}_{\ j}\rangle\rvert_{a=0}=g(\rho,a)\delta^{i}_{\ j},

\displaystyle\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}-\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}\rvert_{a=0}=f-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho f,

\displaystyle\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}-\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}\rvert_{a=0}=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho f,

\displaystyle\langle T_{\rho\psi}\rangle=0=t_{\rho\psi},

(8.18)

where
f(\rho,a)=\mathcal{A}^{(q)}-g(\rho,a)
. In 2 dimensions we have
g(\rho,a)=0
. In
d>2
for a planar entangling boundary, we can always choose
g(\rho,a)=0
(since the manifold is
C_{2}\times\Sigma
and
g_{ij}^{(q)}
has no
a
dependence). Using the coordinate transformation (
3.3
) we have,

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}+\mathcal{A}^{[1]}\sin^{2}(\psi).

(8.19)

8.2.1
2 dimensional CFT

Let us consider a CFT on a 2-dimensional static metric.

ds^{2}_{M_{1}}=g_{\tau\tau}(r)d\tau^{2}+g_{rr}(r)dr^{2},

(8.20)

where
r\in[0,\infty)
,
\tau\in(-\infty,\infty)
. We consider the entangling region
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
.

The replica metric in a local region around the endpoint
r=r_{0}\,(r_{1}\text{ or }r_{2})
is given by

ds^{2}_{M_{q}}\text{ around endpoint}=U(\rho,a)d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2},

(8.21)

where
\rho\in[0,\infty)
and
\psi\in[0,2\pi q)
. We have
(r-r_{0})=\rho\cos\psi
,
(\tau-\tau_{0})=\rho\sin\psi
, and the conical regulating function
U(\rho,a)\rvert_{\rho\to 0}=q^{2}
,
U(\rho,a)\rvert_{\rho>>a}=1
. The non trivial contribution in the difference of replica and base quantities is from locally around the conical singularity.

In 2-dimensions the geometric part of the stress tensor is given by

\chi_{\mu\nu}[g_{\mu\nu}]=\left(n=-\frac{c}{48\pi}\right)Rg_{\mu\nu},

(8.22)

where
c
is the central charge.

Therefore we have (see section (
6.3.1
)),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{48\pi}R^{(q)}g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}.

(8.23)

From the replica metric around the entangling boundary given by (
8.21
), we have

\displaystyle R^{(q)}-R

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\rho}U}{\rho U^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(q)}-\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=2nR^{(q)}=2n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U}{\rho U^{2}}=\left(\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=2n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}}{\rho}\right)(q-1)+O((q-1)^{2}),

\displaystyle\chi_{\rho\rho}^{(q)}-\chi_{\rho\rho}

\displaystyle=n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U}{\rho U}=\left(\chi_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}=n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}}{\rho}\right)(q-1)+O((q-1)^{2}),

\displaystyle\chi_{\psi\psi}^{(q)}-\chi_{\psi\psi}

\displaystyle=n\frac{\rho\partial_{\rho}U}{U^{2}}=\left(\chi_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}=n\rho\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}\right)(q-1)+O((q-1)^{2}),

\displaystyle\chi_{\rho\psi}^{(q)}-\chi_{\rho\psi}

\displaystyle=0.

(8.24)

We have skipped the conformal factor
e^{\sigma}
in the replica metric which captures
O(q-1)^{0}
terms from
M_{1}
as well. This is fine since we are only interested in the
O(q-1)
terms. Notice
\chi^{\rho}_{\rho}{}^{[1]}=\chi^{\psi}_{\psi}{}^{[1]}
.

2 dimensional CFT vacuum state on a flat background:

We will focus on the vacuum state on a flat background. On a flat background we have
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}=0
but
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}\neq 0
.
In a
d=2
CFT, we now consider the regulator dependent part of the
O(q-1)
replica stress tensor sourced by the trace anomaly
\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]
on
M_{q}
. We have

\displaystyle\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{\psi}_{\ \psi}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}=\mathcal{A}^{[1]}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=\frac{2n}{\rho^{2}}\left(\rho\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}-U^{[1]}\right),

\displaystyle\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=\frac{2nU^{[1]}}{\rho^{2}},

\displaystyle\langle T_{\rho\psi}\rangle=0=t_{\rho\psi},

(8.25)

and

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}+\mathcal{A}^{[1]}\sin^{2}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2n}{\rho^{2}}(U^{[1]}\cos(2\psi)+\rho\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}\sin^{2}(\psi)).

(8.26)

Around each boundary/endpoint of the entangling region, the local representation of
F_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
(the superscript is used to indicate that it is an
O(q-1)
coefficient) is given by (see (
5.12
))

\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\,\,\frac{\delta(\rho)}{2\pi q}\int_{M_{q}}(\sqrt{U}\text{ or }1)\rho\,d\rho\,d\psi\,F_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}.

(8.27)

The choice of
\sqrt{g_{q}}
or
\sqrt{g}
in the volume element gives same result in the limit
q\to 1
.

The local representation of
\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}
around each boundary of the entangling region is,

\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}=-2n\delta(\rho).

(8.28)

We have,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg dep}}=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\left(\int_{M_{q}}d^{2}x\sqrt{g}\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\Bigg[\int_{\rho=0,\psi=0}^{\rho=\infty,\psi=2\pi q}\rho d\rho d\psi\Big(-2n(\delta(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{1}}+\delta(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{2}})\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-8\pi n=\frac{c}{6}.

(8.29)

Putting together this and the regulator independent replica stress tensor contribution to EE in 2 dim CFT given by (
8.7
), we have

S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}=\frac{c}{6}\log\left(\frac{\Delta r}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}\right).

(8.30)

This is the well known result for EE of vacuum on a 2 dimensional flat background.

Alternatively we can also look at the decomposition of the
O(q-1)
replica corrections into state and geometric parts. We also have the state part of the replica stress tensor corrections
t_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
given by,

\displaystyle t^{\rho}_{\ \rho}{}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\langle T^{\rho}_{\ \rho}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\chi^{\rho}_{\ \rho}{}^{[1]}=-t^{\psi}_{\ \psi}{}^{[1]}=\frac{C_{2}^{[1]}}{\rho^{2}}+n\left(\frac{2U^{[1]}}{\rho^{2}}-\frac{\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}}{\rho}\right).

(8.31)

Notice that the state part of the replica stress tensor correction
t_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
also has metric dependent terms.

Using the coordinate transformation (
3.3
) we have,

\displaystyle\chi_{rr}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}=n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}}{\rho},

(8.32)

\displaystyle\chi_{r\tau}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=2n\frac{\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}}{\rho}.

(8.34)

Similarly,

\displaystyle t^{r}_{r}{}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-t^{\tau}_{\tau}{}^{[1]}=t^{\rho}_{\ \rho}{}^{[1]}\cos(2\psi),

\displaystyle t^{r}_{\tau}{}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=t^{\tau}_{r}{}^{[1]}=t^{\rho}_{\ \rho}{}^{[1]}\sin(2\psi).

(8.35)

We have
\langle T^{\mu}_{\nu}\rangle{}^{[1]}=t^{\mu}_{\nu}{}^{[1]}+\chi^{\mu}_{\nu}{}^{[1]}
.

The local representation of
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
around each boundary of the entangling region is,

\displaystyle\chi_{rr}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}=-2n\delta(\rho),

(8.36)

\displaystyle\chi_{r\tau}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=-4n\delta(\rho).

(8.38)

Similarly the local representation of
t_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
around each boundary of the entangling region is,

\displaystyle t^{r}_{r}{}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=t^{\tau}_{\tau}{}^{[1]}=t^{r}_{\tau}{}^{[1]}=0.

(8.39)

In flat space vacuum CFT state we have
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}=t_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}=0
, therefore

\chi^{\mu}_{\nu}{}^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[1]}\chi_{\alpha\nu}^{[0]}+g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}\chi_{\alpha\nu}^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}\chi_{\alpha\nu}^{[1]},\\
t^{\mu}_{\nu}{}^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[1]}t_{\alpha\nu}^{[0]}+g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}t_{\alpha\nu}^{[1]}=g^{\mu\alpha}{}^{[0]}t_{\alpha\nu}^{[1]}.

We have,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial\Delta r}=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\left(\int_{M_{q}}d^{2}x\sqrt{g}\chi^{r}_{r}{}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\Bigg[\int_{\rho=0,\psi=0}^{\rho=\infty,\psi=2\pi q}\rho d\rho d\psi\Big(-2n(\delta(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{1}}+\delta(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{2}})\Big)\Bigg]

(8.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-8\pi n=\frac{c}{6}.

(8.41)

which implies

S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}=\frac{c}{6}\log\left(\frac{\Delta r}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}\right).

(8.42)

Massive scalar field on a flat background:

In 2 dimensional QFTs on flat space, in the presence of mass
m
the regulator-independent part of the replica stress tensor (
7.20
) has an additional
m
dependent term which additionally contributes to the EE as can be seen in (
8.7
),

\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\frac{c}{6}+\frac{\pi}{4}m^{2}(M_{2}^{[1]}+N_{2}^{[1]})(\Delta r)^{2},

(8.43)

which implies

S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}=\frac{c}{6}\log\left(\frac{\Delta r}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}\right)+\frac{\pi}{8}m^{2}(M_{2}^{[1]}+N_{2}^{[1]})(\Delta r)^{2}.

(8.44)

Similarly for the fermionic field one has to use the additional contributions in the regulator-independent part of the replica stress tensor given in
[
59
]
.

2 dimensional CFT on a general background:

In case of a general metric, we have

\displaystyle-\alpha\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial r_{1}}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g^{rr}\langle T_{rr}\rangle^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\tau\tau}\langle T_{\tau\tau}\rangle^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g^{rr}\chi_{rr}^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\tau\tau}\chi_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}\right)+\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g^{rr}t_{rr}^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\tau\tau}t_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}\rho d\rho d\psi\alpha\Big(2g^{rr}+(r-r_{2})\partial_{r}g^{rr}+(r-r_{2})\partial_{r}g^{\tau\tau}\Big)\Big(-2n(\delta(\rho)\rvert_{r_{1}}+\delta(\rho)\rvert_{r_{2}})\Big)

\displaystyle+\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g^{rr}t_{rr}^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\tau\tau}t_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi n\left\{2(g^{rr}(r_{1})+g^{rr}(r_{2}))+(r_{1}-r_{2})\partial_{r}g^{rr}\rvert_{r_{1}}+(r_{1}-r_{2})\partial_{r}g^{\tau\tau}\rvert_{r_{1}}\right\}

\displaystyle+\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\delta_{\Delta r}g^{rr}t_{rr}^{[1]}+\delta_{\Delta r}g^{\tau\tau}t_{\tau\tau}^{[1]}\right).

(8.45)

For the state dependent part one has to solve (
7.2
)-(
7.6
), where we have a non zero source
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}
and in the case of general state
t_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}
. Notice that the scaling of EE above is not wrt
\Delta r
, but wrt the boundary coordinate
r_{1}
. This reflects the lack of translation symmetry along
r
, on a general metric.

8.2.2
Even dimensional CFT

In odd dimensional CFTs since the trace anomaly
\mathcal{A}[g_{\mu\nu}]=0=\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]
therefore there are no regulator dependent contributions to EE. The scaling of the EE is completely given by (
8.7
).

In even dimensional CFTs the trace anomaly on
M_{1}
is non zero and is given by (
6.24
). The anomaly on the replica manifold
\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]
is given by the same expression but with
g_{\mu\nu}
replaced by
g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(d,q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]=\left\langle\chi_{\ \mu}^{\mu}\right\rangle_{d,q}=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\left(\sum_{j}c_{dj}I_{j}^{(d,q)}-(-)^{\frac{d}{2}}a_{d}E^{(d,q)}+\sum_{j}d_{dj}D^{i}J_{i}^{(d,q)}\right).

(8.46)

In general, the replica metric in this case locally around the entangling boundary is given by (
3.11
),

ds_{q}^{2}\text{ expanded around boundary}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},reg}=g^{(q)}_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}|_{bdy}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}U(\rho,a)}d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2}+\delta_{ij}dz^{i}dz^{j}.

(8.47)

We notice that the replica metric has no
\psi
dependence and hence the replica anomaly in any even dimensional CFT, for this replica metric, is also independent of
\psi
. From (
8.19
), we have

\displaystyle\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}+\mathcal{A}^{[1]}\sin^{2}(\psi).

(8.48)

Using (
5.12
), the local representation of
\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}
around each boundary of the entangling region is,

\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}=\frac{\delta_{\Sigma}(\rho)\pi q}{Vol(M_{d-2})}\int_{M_{q}}d\rho d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=N\delta_{\Sigma}(\rho).

(8.49)

We have,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{d}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg dep}}=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\left(\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}-\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle{}^{[1]}\rvert_{a=0}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\Bigg[N\int_{M_{q}}\rho d\rho d\psi d^{d-2}\sqrt{h}\Big(\delta_{\Sigma}(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{1}}+\delta_{\Sigma}(\rho)\rvert_{\text{around }r_{2}}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{M_{q}}d\rho d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=2\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\int_{0}^{\infty}d\rho\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]},

(8.50)

where in the last equality we have made use of the fact that we are dealing with a planar entangling region and hence
g_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
and
\mathcal{A}^{[1]}
are independent of
y^{i}{}^{\prime}s
.

2 dimensions:

See (
8.29
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=-\frac{c}{24}R^{[1]}\implies\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg dep}}=\frac{c}{6}.

(8.51)

4 dimensions:

\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]=\chi_{\ \mu}^{\mu}[g_{\mu\nu}]^{d=4}=-\frac{a}{(4\pi)^{2}}E^{(q)}_{4}+\frac{c}{(4\pi)^{2}}I^{(q)}_{4},

(8.52)

where
I^{(q)}_{4}
is the term involving weyl contraction
C^{(q)}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}C^{(q)}{}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
.

\displaystyle E_{4}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=R^{(q)}_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}R^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}{}^{(q)}-4R^{(q)}_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}{}^{(q)}+R^{(q)}{}^{2},

\displaystyle I_{4}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=R^{(q)}_{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}R^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}{}^{(q)}-2R^{(q)}_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}{}^{(q)}+\frac{1}{3}R^{(q)}{}^{2}.

(8.53)

We make use of known integrals of Riemann contractions on replica metric i.e., (
5.32
), (
5.35
) to get,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{d}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg dep}}=

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{M_{q}}d\rho d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(aI^{\Sigma}_{a}-cI^{\Sigma}_{c}).

(8.54)

This is the universal logarithmic contribution to
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
. For a planar entangling region on flat space the Ricci scalar of the background, the extrinsic curvature of
\Sigma
and projection of Ricci and Riemann tensors on
\Sigma
is zero, therefore (
8.54
) is zero.

5 and 6 dimensions:

We can similarly compute the scaling of regulator-dependent contribution to EE in higher dimensions by using (
8.50
). The replica trace anomaly is given by (
6.24
) with
g_{\mu\nu}
replaced by
g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}
. The relevant replica integrals in 5 and 6 dimensions are listed in
[
44
]
.

The replica anomaly is independent of the choice of the CFT state. Therefore, the results in this section hold for any CFT state.

General deformation:

So far we have considered a particular kind of scaling i.e., we scale the entangling region along its finite length direction. Consider now (
4.18
) where we consider a general deformation of the background metric and the entangling surface geometry captured by
\delta_{L}g_{\mu\nu}
. For the CFT replica stress tensor given by (
7.20
) (i.e., all other coefficients except
C_{d}(q)
is zero), it was shown in
[
39
]
that two terms in (
3.26
) contribute to logarithmic terms in
\delta_{L}S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
in 4-dimensions,

\delta_{L}g_{ij}=x^{a}x^{c}R_{iacj};\quad\delta_{L}g_{ab}=\frac{-1}{3}x^{c}x^{d}R_{acbd}.

(8.55)

We have,

\displaystyle\delta_{L}S_{\textnormal{QFT}}=\frac{c}{6\pi}\int d^{2}y(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}R_{abcd}+\delta^{ij}\delta^{ac}R_{iacj})\log(l/\epsilon).

(8.56)

here
l
is the characteristic length scale of perturbations and
\epsilon
is a UV cutoff. We have made use of (
7.35
). (
7.36
) can be written as
C_{T}=40c/\pi^{4}
where
c
is the CFT central charge corresponding to the Weyl tensor contractions in the 4-dimensional trace anomaly. This should be compared with Solodukhin’s formula
[
43
]
for the universal part of entanglement entropy in the case of a 4-dimensional CFT.

8.3
General QFT

The replica stress tensor vev
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[1]}
for a planar entangling boundary in a general unitary
d
dimensional field theory (free or interacting) was given in
[
54
]

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{ij}\left(x_{1},x_{2},y\right)\right\rangle^{[1]}=

\displaystyle-\left\langle T_{ij}\left(x_{1},x_{2},y\right)K_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{d}\delta_{ij}}{(d-1)^{2}\Gamma(d)}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\mu\left(c^{(0)}(\mu)-(d-1)c^{(2)}(\mu)\right)\mu^{2}K_{0}(\mu\rho),

(8.57)

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2},y\right)\right\rangle^{[1]}=

\displaystyle-\left\langle T_{ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2},y\right)K_{0}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{d}}{(d-1)^{2}\Gamma(d)}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\mu\left(c^{(0)}(\mu)+(d-1)(d-2)c^{(2)}(\mu)\right)

\displaystyle\left(\mu^{2}K_{0}(\mu\rho)\left(\delta_{ab}-\frac{x_{a}x_{b}}{\rho^{2}}\right)+\frac{\mu K_{1}(\mu\rho)}{\rho}\left(\delta_{ab}-\frac{2x_{a}x_{b}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\right).

(8.58)

where
x_{a}
with
a=1,2
are transverse to the entangling boundary and
y^{i}{}^{\prime}s
are along the entangling boundary.
\mu
is the spectral parameter and
c^{(0)}(\mu)
and
c^{(2)}(\mu)
are spectral functions that encode the information of the exact QFT, and
A_{d}=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{(d+1)2^{d-2}\Gamma(d/2)}
.

One could easily repeat the calculation for EE scaling that we just did for a CFT, for a
d-
dimensional field theory using the expressions above. We have,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg indep}}

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{\Sigma}d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\int_{0}^{2\pi q}d\psi\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\rho\langle T^{r}_{r}\rangle_{a=0}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}Vol(M_{d-2})\int_{0}^{2\pi q}d\psi\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\Bigg(\frac{A_{d}}{(d-1)^{2}\Gamma(d)}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\mu C(\mu)

\displaystyle\left(\mu^{2}\rho K_{0}(\mu\rho)\sin^{2}(\psi)-\mu K_{1}(\mu\rho)\cos(2\psi)\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi A_{d}Vol(M_{d-2})}{(d-1)^{2}\Gamma(d)}\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}d\mu C(\mu)\mu^{2}\rho K_{0}(\mu\rho)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\pi A_{d}Vol(M_{d-2})}{(d-1)^{2}\Gamma(d)}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}d\mu C(\mu)(F(\mu,(r_{2}-r_{1})/2)-F(\mu,\epsilon))\Bigg),

(8.59)

with
F(\mu,\rho)=\mu\rho K_{1}(\mu\rho)
,
C(\mu)=\left(c^{(0)}(\mu)+(d-1)(d-2)c^{(2)}(\mu)\right)
. In the CFT limit
c^{(0)}(\mu)\propto\mu^{d-2}\delta(\mu)
and
c^{(2)}(\mu)=C_{T}\frac{d-1}{d}\mu^{d-3}
, this reproduces the CFT result we have in (
7.20
), and (
8.7
) with

C_{d}^{[1]}=-\frac{C_{T}\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma(d+2)},

(8.60)

where
C_{T}
is the charge that appears in the 2 point energy momentum correlation function in a
d-
dimensional CFT.

8.4
Entangling boundary with extrinsic curvature

Consider a general CFT state on flat space on
M_{1}
with a spherical or cylindrical entangling boundary i.e.,

ds^{2}_{M_{1},sph}=-dt^{2}+dr^{2}+r^{2}h_{ij}(y)dy^{i}dy^{j},

(8.61)

with entangling region
r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}
spanning the sphere directions
y^{i}
on a constant
t
slice.

or

ds^{2}_{M_{1},cyl}=-dt^{2}+dr^{2}+r^{2}d\phi^{2}+dz^{2},

(8.62)

with entangling region
r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}
spanning
\phi
and
\frac{-L}{2}\leq z\leq\frac{L}{2}
on a constant
t
slice.

The replica metric locally around the boundary of entangling region
r=r_{0}\in\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
is given by (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) i.e.,

\displaystyle ds_{q}^{2}{}_{\text{ around }\Sigma,sph}

\displaystyle=U(\rho)d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2}+(r_{0}+\rho^{p}c^{1-p}\cos(\psi))^{2}h_{ij}(y)dy^{i}dy^{j},

\displaystyle ds_{q}^{2}{}_{\text{ around }\Sigma,cyl}

\displaystyle=U(\rho)d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2}+(r_{0}+\rho^{p}c^{1-p}\cos(\psi))^{2}d\phi^{2}+dz^{2}.

(8.63)

Using (
5.51
), the scaling of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
in this case is given by,

\alpha\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\,\frac{1}{2}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\,\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu}\langle T^{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[1]}

(8.64)

Since
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
depends only on
\Delta r
due to translation symmetry along
r
, we consider the scaling of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
wrt
\Delta r\to e^{\alpha}\Delta r
or
r\to e^{\alpha}r
. On scaling the metric (
8.61
) and (
8.62
), we get

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu,sph}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu,sph},\quad\mu=\nu=r,\text{ and }\mu,\nu\in\{i,j\},

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu,cyl}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu,cyl},\quad\mu=\nu=r\text{ or }\phi,

(8.65)

therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim, sph}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left(\langle T^{rr}\rangle^{[1]}+r^{2}h_{ij}\langle T^{ij}\rangle^{[1]}\right),

(8.66)

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim,cyl}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{4}x\sqrt{g}\left(\langle T^{rr}\rangle^{[1]}+r^{2}\langle T^{\phi\phi}\rangle^{[1]}\right).

(8.67)

If we consider flat space and vacuum state, we have symmetry along
\tau
and
-L/2\leq z\leq L/2
, therefore
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
is also independent of
\tau
and
z
. We will restrict to vacuum state and scale
\tau
and
z
as well. We get

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu,sph}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu,sph},

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu,cyl}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu,cyl}.

(8.68)

Therefore, for the vacuum state, we have

\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\,\eta_{\mu\nu}\langle T^{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[1]}.

(8.69)

Furthermore, for vacuum state we have
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[0]}=0
, which implies
\eta_{\mu\nu}\langle T^{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[1]}=\langle T^{\mu}_{\mu}\rangle^{[1]}
. This implies

\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\langle T^{\mu}_{\mu}\rangle^{[1]}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\mathcal{A}^{[1]}.

(8.70)

As we see from the expressions above the only contribution to EE scaling for a spherical or cylindrical entangling region on flat background is from the regulator dependent replica anomaly
\mathcal{A}^{(q)}
at
O(q-1)
. There are no regulator independent replica stress tensor contributions, that were present in the case of planar disconnected entangling boundaries.

The replica anomaly contribution above is very similar to the regulator dependent planar contribution (
6.24
), except now the replica metric (
8.63
) and hence the replica anomaly can depend on the coordinate
\psi
and have non zero extrinsic curvature for boundaries
r=r_{0}
. These integrals were performed in
[
44
]
and we have listed them in (
5.32
), (
5.33
), (
5.34
).

2 dimensions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{[1]}=-\frac{c}{24}R^{[1]}\implies\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{2}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg dep}}=\frac{c}{6}.

(8.71)

4 dimensions:

\mathcal{A}^{(q)}[g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}]=\chi_{\ \mu}^{\mu}[g_{\mu\nu}]^{d=4}=-\frac{a}{(4\pi)^{2}}E^{(q)}_{4}+\frac{c}{(4\pi)^{2}}I^{(q)}_{4}.

(8.72)

Using known integrals of Riemann contractions on replica metric with extrinsic curvature i.e., (
5.32
), (
5.35
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{4}}}{\partial\Delta r}=

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{M_{q}}d\rho d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\rho\mathcal{A}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(aI^{\Sigma}_{a}-cI^{\Sigma}_{c}).

(8.73)

For a spherical and cylindrical
\Sigma
on flat space we only have contributions from integrals of
K^{2}
and
\text{Tr }K^{2}
. Using the value of these integrals in both cases given by (
5.33
), (
5.34
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{4}}}{\partial\Delta r}=

\displaystyle 4a;\quad\text{Spherical $\Sigma$},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{L}{2r_{0}}c;\quad\text{Cylindrical $\Sigma$}.

(8.74)

at each entangling boundary. Therefore, the net contribution in our case would be the sum of the contributions from the two disconnected entangling boundaries at
r_{0}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
. The spherical
\Sigma
isolates central charge
a
contribution and the cylindrical
\Sigma
isolates central charge
c
contribution.

5 and 6 dimensions:

We can similarly compute the scaling of EE in higher dimensions by using (
8.70
). The replica trace anomaly is given by (
6.24
) with
g_{\mu\nu}
replaced by
g_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}
. The relevant replica integrals in 5 and 6 dimensions are listed in
[
44
]
.

9
Entanglement entropy - non vacuum states

9.1
Thermal state in odd dimensional CFTs

Odd dimensional CFTs do not have an anomaly, and more generally
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{[0]}=0=\chi_{\mu\nu}^{(q)}
. We consider this case with static metrics on
M_{1}
satisfying
\Gamma^{i}_{k\mu}=0
,
\mu\in\{\rho,\psi\}
. This is true for planar entangling region in flat space on
M_{1}

ds^{2}_{M_{1}}=d\tau^{2}+dr^{2}+\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}.

(9.1)

It is also true for a CFT on a
d-
dimensional static metric with a finite volume along the
x^{i}
directions

ds^{2}_{M_{1}}=g_{\tau\tau}(r)d\tau^{2}+g_{rr}(r)dr^{2}+\phi_{0}^{2}(d\Omega_{d-2}^{2}=h_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}),

(9.2)

where
r\in[0,\infty)
,
\tau\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
M_{d-2}
is compact.

We consider the usual entangling region
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
at
\tau=\tau_{0}
spanning the
x^{i}
directions.

The replica metric in a local region around the boundary
r=r_{0}\,(r_{1}\text{ or }r_{2})
is given by

ds^{2}_{M_{q}}\text{ around endpoint}=U(\rho,a)d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\psi^{2}+\phi_{0}^{2}h_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j},

(9.3)

where
(r-r_{0})=\rho\cos\psi
,
(\tau-\tau_{0})=\rho\sin\psi
, and the conical regulating function
U(\rho,a)\rvert_{\rho\to 0}\to q^{2}
,
U(\rho,a)\rvert_{\rho>>a}\to 1
. We could always use coordinate
x=\rho/a
where
U(x)
is independent of regulator a. We have
\Gamma^{i}_{i\rho}{}^{(q)}=0=\Gamma^{i}_{i\psi}{}^{(q)}
.

9.1.1
Replica stress tensor

The replica trace and conservation equations (
6.62
)-(
6.65
), (
6.55
) reduce to

O(q-1)
Traceless condition:

t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}+t^{i}_{\ i}{}^{[1]}=U^{[1]}(\rho,a)t_{\rho\rho}^{[0]}.

(9.4)

O(q-1)

\rho
-component of Conservation:
(which includes trace condition)

\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\psi}t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\rho}t^{[1]}_{\psi\psi}-\left(\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}\right)t^{i}_{\ i}{}^{[1]}=0.

(9.5)

O(q-1)

\psi
-component of Conservation:

\left(\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}\right)t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\psi}t^{[1]}_{\psi\psi}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}+U^{[1]}\partial_{\rho}+\frac{U^{[1]}}{\rho}\right)t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}.

(9.6)

O(q-1)

i
-component of Conservation:

\text{For a fixed $i$: }(\partial_{i}+\sum_{l}\Gamma^{l}_{\ li})\langle T^{i}_{\ i}\rangle^{[1]}-\sum_{l}\Gamma^{l}_{\ li}\langle T^{l}_{\ l}\rangle^{[1]}=0.

(9.7)

We will now calculate the scaling of the EE in this case. When the metric on
M_{1}
is flat, scaling the metric using (
5.40
), we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\Delta r}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\alpha;\quad\mu=\nu=r,

(9.8)

\displaystyle=0;\quad\text{otherwise}.

(9.9)

Therefore using (
5.51
), the scaling of the EE is given by,

\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{d}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}\,t^{rr}{}^{[1]}.

(9.10)

Using the coordinate transformation (
3.3
) we have,

\displaystyle t^{rr}

\displaystyle=U^{-2}t_{\rho\rho}^{(q)}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2U^{-1}}{\rho}t_{\rho\psi}^{(q)}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t_{\psi\psi}^{(q)}(\sin(\psi))^{2},

(9.11)

\displaystyle t^{rr}{}^{[1]}\Big|_{\text{around }r_{0},\tau_{0}}

\displaystyle=t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2}{\rho}t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}(\sin(\psi))^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt-2U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2U^{[1]}}{\rho}t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi).

(9.12)

We will consider the CFT thermal stress tensor on a flat background ((
6.71
) with
\epsilon=-(d+1)p
and
g_{rr}=g_{\tau\tau}=1
,
g_{r\tau}=0
,
g_{ij}=\phi_{0}^{2}h_{ij}
)

\displaystyle t_{\rho\psi}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=(-dp/2)\rho\sin(2\psi),

\displaystyle t_{\rho\rho}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=p[1-d(\sin(\psi))^{2}],

\displaystyle t_{\psi\psi}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=p\rho^{2}[1-d(\cos(\psi))^{2}],

\displaystyle t_{ij}^{[0]}

\displaystyle=p(g_{ij}=\phi_{0}^{2}h_{ij}\text{ or }\delta_{ij}).

(9.13)

Homogenous solution:

Let us first consider the homogenous solution to the replica trace and conservation equations above. (
9.7
) has no sources and has the solution

t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}=\frac{(d-2)C^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}+t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}.

(9.14)

The
\frac{C^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}
term reflects the lack of
\rho
translation symmetry due to the conical singularity on the replica manifold. Since the
i-
conservation equations on
M_{1}
and
M_{q}
are the same with
t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}\to t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}
, noting (
6.1
) we additionally have the
t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}
term.

Noting that
t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}
and
t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}
are independent of
\psi
, (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) implies,

\left(1+3\rho\partial_{\rho}+\rho^{2}\partial_{\rho}^{2}+\partial_{\psi}^{2}\right)t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}=0.

(9.15)

We use the ansatz
t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}=A\rho^{\Delta}m(\psi)
, This gives

\displaystyle\partial_{\psi}^{2}m(\psi)=-k^{2}m(\psi);\quad k^{2}=(\Delta+1)^{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\Delta=(-k-1)\text{ or }(k-1),

(9.16)

which implies

\displaystyle m(\psi)=c_{1}\cos(k\psi)+c_{2}\sin(k\psi).

(9.17)

We have the following replica corrections to the replica stress tensor,

t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}=\sum_{k}(A_{k}\rho^{-(k+1)}+B_{k}\rho^{(k-1)})(c_{1}\cos(k\psi)+c_{2}\sin(k\psi)).

(9.18)

Noting our observations in (
6.1
), we must have
t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}=t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}
which implies
B_{2}c_{2}=-dp/2
,
c_{1}=0
,
A_{k}=0

\forall\,k
and
B_{k\neq 2}=0
. With this we have

t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}=\frac{-dp}{2}\rho\sin(2\psi).

(9.19)

(
9.5
) implies

\displaystyle t^{[1]}_{\psi\psi}

\displaystyle=\int d\rho\Bigg[\partial_{\psi}t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}-\rho^{2}\left(\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{\rho}\right)t^{i}_{\ i}{}^{[1]}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(B_{k}\rho^{k}-A_{k}\rho^{-k})(c_{2}\cos(k\psi)-c_{1}\sin(k\psi))+(1-d)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}}+\frac{(2-d)}{2}p\rho^{2}+c_{3}(\psi)

\displaystyle=[t^{[0]}_{\psi\psi}=p\rho^{2}(1-d\cos^{2}(\psi))]+(1-d)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}},

(9.20)

where we have chosen
c_{3}(\psi)=0
on dimensional grounds.
(
9.4
) implies,

\displaystyle t^{[1]}_{\rho\rho}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t^{[1]}_{\psi\psi}-t^{i}_{\ i}{}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(A\rho^{-k-2}-B\rho^{k-2})(c_{2}\cos(k\psi)-c_{1}\sin(k\psi))

\displaystyle=[t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}=p\rho^{2}(1-d\sin^{2}(\psi))]+\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}.

(9.21)

Notice that
\partial_{\psi}
is a killing vector on
M_{1}
and
M_{q}
but the thermal state breaks this symmetry. Considering this and the discussion in (
6.1
), the homogenous solution to
t^{[1]}_{\mu\nu}
contains the vacuum part which has
\psi
translation symmetry and the thermal homogenous part which does not have this symmetry i.e.,

t^{[1]}_{\mu\nu}\rvert_{homogenous}=\frac{C^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}((1-d)\rho^{2},1,1,1,1,...,1)+t^{[0]}_{\mu\nu}(\rho,\psi)\rvert_{thermal},

(9.22)

where the basis is in order
\{\psi,\rho,x^{i}\}
.

Full solution:

Let us consider a general CFT state. Combining (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) into a second order PDE in
t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}
only,

\displaystyle(\partial_{\psi}^{2}+\rho^{2}\partial_{\rho}^{2}+3\rho\partial_{\rho}+1)t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}(\rho,\psi)=\Bigg(\left(1+2\rho\partial_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}\partial_{\rho}^{2}\right)U^{[1]}(\rho,a)

\displaystyle+\left(3\rho U^{[1]}(\rho,a)+\frac{3}{2}\rho^{2}\partial_{\rho}U^{[1]}(\rho,a)\right)\partial_{\rho}+\rho^{2}U^{[1]}(\rho,a)\partial_{\rho}^{2}\Bigg)t_{\rho\psi}^{[0]}(\rho,\psi),

(9.23)

We will now demand

\displaystyle t_{\rho\psi}^{[0]}=h(\rho)m(\psi),

(9.24)

\displaystyle\partial_{\psi}^{2}m(\psi)=-n^{2}m(\psi)\text{ i.e., }m(\psi)=c_{1}\cos(n\psi)+c_{2}\sin(n\psi),

(9.25)

for arbitrary constants
n
,
c_{1},c_{2}
.

The solution to (
9.23
) is

t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}(\rho,\psi)=g(\rho)m(\psi),

(9.26)

where
g(\rho)
satisifies the first-order ODE

\displaystyle\rho\left(2\rho g^{\prime\prime}(\rho)+6g^{\prime}(\rho)\right)-2(n^{2}-1)g(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2U^{[1]}(\rho,a)\rho^{2}h^{\prime\prime}(\rho)+3\left(\rho^{2}U^{[1]}{{}^{\prime}}(\rho,a)+2\rho U^{[1]}(\rho,a)\right)h^{\prime}(\rho)

\displaystyle+h(\rho)\left(\rho\left(\rho U^{[1]}{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\rho,a)+4U^{[1]}{{}^{\prime}}(\rho,a)\right)+2U^{[1]}(\rho,a)\right).

(9.27)

This can be solved for
f(\rho)
but we will not write the solution here.

(
9.24
) is not true for a general CFT state, however,it is true for the CFT thermal stress tensor on a flat background (
9.13
). Restricting further to this state i.e.,
h(\rho)=-dp\rho/2
and
m(\psi)=\sin(2\psi)
(i.e., a particular solution to (
9.25
) with
n=2
), we have

\displaystyle t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}=\Bigg\{\frac{d_{1}}{\rho^{3}}+d_{2}\rho+\frac{dp}{16}af(x)\Bigg\}\sin(2\psi),

(9.28)

with

\displaystyle f(x)=x\Bigg(2U^{[1]}(x)+xU^{[1]}{{}^{\prime}}(x)-\int_{1}^{x}\left(\frac{8U^{[1]}(x^{\prime})}{x^{\prime}}+7U^{[1]}{{}^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})+x^{\prime}U^{[1]}{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x^{\prime})\right)dx^{\prime}\Bigg),

(9.29)

where
x=\rho/a
.
d_{1},d_{2}
are integration constants. Comparing with the homogenous solution (
9.19
) and noting (
6.1
) we get

\displaystyle d_{1}=0;\quad d_{2}=\frac{-dp}{2},

(9.30)

i.e.,

t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}=t_{\rho\psi}^{[0]}+\frac{dp}{16}af(x)\sin(2\psi).

(9.31)

(
9.7
) has no sources and has the same solution as the homogenous solution on
M_{q}

t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}=\frac{(d-2)C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}+t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}.

(9.32)

Using (
9.5
),

\displaystyle t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\int d\rho\partial_{\psi}t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}-\int d\rho\rho\partial_{\rho}(\rho t^{i}{}_{i}{}^{[1]})

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\frac{-d_{1}}{\rho^{2}}+d_{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{dp}{8}a^{2}\int dxf(x)\Bigg\}\cos(2\psi)+d_{3}(\psi)-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}(t^{i}{}_{i}{}^{[1]}=t^{i}{}_{i}{}^{[0]}=(d-2)p)

\displaystyle=(1-d)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}}+t_{\psi\psi}^{[0]}+\frac{dp}{8}a^{2}\int dxf(x)\cos(2\psi)+d_{3}(\psi).

(9.33)

We again choose
d_{3}(\psi)=0
on dimensional grounds. Using (
9.4
)

\displaystyle t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}=

\displaystyle-\frac{t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}}{\rho^{2}}-t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}+U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\frac{d_{1}}{\rho^{4}}-d_{2}-\frac{dp}{8x^{2}}\int dxf(x)\Bigg\}\cos(2\psi)-\frac{t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}}{2}+U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}+t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}-\frac{dp}{8x^{2}}\int dxf(x)\cos(2\psi)+U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}.

(9.34)

We have,

\displaystyle t^{rr}{}^{[1]}=

\displaystyle t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2}{\rho}t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}(\sin(\psi))^{2}

\displaystyle-2U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2U^{[1]}}{\rho}t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}(1-d\sin^{2}(\psi))+t_{rr}^{[0]}=\left(p\left(1-\frac{d}{2}\right)-d_{2}+d_{1}\frac{\cos(4\psi)}{\rho^{4}}\right)+

\displaystyle I(x,\psi)=\Bigg[\left(U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}-\frac{dp}{8x^{2}}\int dxf(x)\cos(2\psi)\right)\cos(\psi)^{2}-\left(\frac{dp}{16x}f(x)\sin^{2}(2\psi)\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{dp}{8x^{2}}\int dxf(x)\cos(2\psi)\right)\sin(\psi)^{2}-2U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2U^{[1]}}{\rho}t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)\Bigg].

(9.35)

The universal parts of
U^{[1]}(x)
is determined by
(
3.10
)
. We have
U^{[1]}(x)\rvert_{x\to 0}=2
,
U^{[1]}(x)\rvert_{x>>1}=0
. Notice that
t_{\mu\nu}^{[1]}
with
\mu,\nu\in\{\rho,\psi\}
depends on non-universal terms in the regulating function i.e., it seems to depend on the choice of the regulator. We will work with a specific regulating function (
3.7
) i.e.,
U(x,a)=\frac{x^{2}+q^{2}}{x^{2}+1}
with
U^{[1]}(x,a)=\frac{2}{1+x^{2}}
. With this choice, we have,

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=8x\left(\log\left(1+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)-\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\right),

(9.36)

\displaystyle\int f(x)dx

\displaystyle=8x^{2}\log\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{x^{2}}}\right);\quad x=\frac{\rho}{a}.

(9.37)

9.1.2
Entanglement Entropy

The scaling of the EE in this case is given by,

\displaystyle\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{CFT}_{d}}}{\partial\Delta r}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}t_{rr}^{[1]}

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1,a\to 0}{lim}\int_{M_{d-2},\psi=0}^{2\pi q}\sqrt{h}d^{d-2}y^{i}d\psi\Bigg[\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\rho\Bigg(\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}[1-d\sin^{2}(\psi)]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(t^{[0]}_{rr}=p\left(1-\frac{d}{2}\right)-d_{2}+d_{1}\frac{\cos(4\psi)}{\rho^{4}}\right)\Bigg)+a^{2}\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}dxxI(x,\psi)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1,a\to 0}{lim}\pi qVol(M_{d-2})\Bigg[\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}}d\rho\rho\left((2-d)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-1}}+(2-d)p-2d_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+a^{2}p\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}dxx\left(\frac{2(d-1)}{1+x^{2}}-d\log\left(1+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\left\{C_{d}^{[1]}\left(\frac{2^{d-2}}{(\Delta r)^{d-2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d-2}}\right)+\frac{(2-d)p-2d_{2}}{8}(\Delta r)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\left\{C_{d}^{[1]}\left(\frac{2^{d-2}}{(\Delta r)^{d-2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d-2}}\right)+\frac{p}{4}(\Delta r)^{2}\right\}.

(9.38)

We have kept
d_{1},d_{2}
arbitrary until the last equality where we have used (
9.30
). As mentioned in (
6.2
), we restrict the
\rho
integral between
0
to
\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}
for the regulator-independent homogenous replica stress tensor contributions, and between
0
to
\infty
for the regulator dependent replica stress tensor contributions.

We have chosen a specific regulating function
U(x,a)
. But from the second equality above, we see that the
a
dependence in the regulator-dependent integral goes as
a^{2}
, and hence the integral goes to zero as
a\to 0
independent of the choice of
U(x,a)
. So the contribution to the scaling of EE is purely from the regulator independent homogenous part of the replica stress tensor.

When solving the replica trace and conservation equations (
9.4
)-(
9.7
) in the case of a disconnected boundary
\Delta r=L=(r_{2}-r_{1})
distance apart, we can consider
L
to be one more dimensionful parameter that the replica stress tensor
\langle T^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\rangle_{q}
can in general depend on. If we follow an approach similar to the vacuum case we find that the stress tensor and EE change as follows

t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}=\frac{(d-2)C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho/L)+t^{i}_{i}{}^{[0]}K_{1}(\rho/L),

(9.39)

where
K(\rho/L)
and
K_{1}(\rho/L)
are dimensionless functions which tend to 1 as
\rho>>L
, which implies

K(\rho,L)=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{i}\left(\frac{L}{\rho}\right)^{i};\quad K_{1}(\rho,L)=\sum_{i\geq 0}A^{(1)}_{i}\left(\frac{L}{\rho}\right)^{i};\quad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

(9.40)

\begin{split}t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}=\Bigg\{\frac{d_{1}}{\rho^{3}}+d_{2}\rho+\frac{dp}{16}af(x)\Bigg\}\sin(2\psi),\end{split}

(9.41)

\begin{split}&t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}=\int d\rho\partial_{\psi}t^{[1]}_{\rho\psi}-\int d\rho\rho\partial_{\rho}(\rho t^{i}{}_{i}{}^{[1]})\\
&=\Bigg\{\frac{-d_{1}}{\rho^{2}}+d_{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{dp}{8}a^{2}\int dxf(x)\Bigg\}\cos(2\psi)+d_{3}(\psi)-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}(d-2)pK_{1}(\rho/L)\\
&\quad-(d-2)p\int d\rho\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\partial_{\rho}K_{1}(\rho/L)+\frac{(1-d)C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}}K(\rho,L)+\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}},\end{split}

(9.42)

\begin{split}t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}=&-\frac{t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}}{\rho^{2}}-t^{i}_{i}{}^{[1]}+U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}\\
=&\Bigg\{\frac{d_{1}}{\rho^{4}}-d_{2}-\frac{dp}{8x^{2}}\int dxf(x)\Bigg\}\cos(2\psi)+U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}-\frac{(d-2)}{2}pK_{1}(\rho/L)\\
&+\frac{(d-2)p}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\partial_{\rho}K_{1}(\rho/L)+\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho,L)-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}},\end{split}

(9.43)

\begin{split}t_{rr}^{[1]}=&t_{\rho\rho}^{[1]}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2}{\rho}t_{\rho\psi}^{[1]}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}t_{\psi\psi}^{[1]}(\sin(\psi))^{2}\\
&-2U^{[1]}t^{[0]}_{\rho\rho}(\cos(\psi))^{2}-\frac{2U^{[1]}}{\rho}t^{[0]}_{\rho\psi}\cos(\psi)\sin(\psi)\\
=&\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}K(\rho/L)(1-d\sin^{2}(\psi))+p\left(1-\frac{d}{2}\right)K_{1}(\rho/L)-d_{2}+d_{1}\frac{\cos(4\psi)}{\rho^{4}}\\
&+I(x,\psi)+\frac{\cos(2\psi)}{\rho^{2}}\int d\rho\left((d-2)p\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\partial_{\rho}K_{1}(\rho/L)-\partial_{\rho}K(\rho,L)\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d-2}}\right).\end{split}

We have,

\begin{split}&\Delta r\frac{\partial S_{\text{$d$ dim, bulk}}}{\partial\Delta r}\Bigg\rvert_{\text{reg indep}}=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{M_{q}}d^{d}x\sqrt{g}t_{rr}^{[1]}\\
&=\underset{q\to 1}{lim}\int_{\Sigma}d^{d-2}x\sqrt{h}\int_{0}^{2\pi q}d\psi\int_{\epsilon}^{\alpha L}d\rho\rho\Bigg(\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{\rho^{d}}K\left(\frac{\rho}{L}\right)(1-d\sin^{2}(\psi))+p\left(1-\frac{d}{2}\right)K_{1}\left(\frac{\rho}{L}\right)-d_{2}\Bigg)\\
&=\pi Vol(M_{d-2})\Bigg[(2-d)\sum_{i\geq 0}\Bigg(\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}}{L^{d-2}}\frac{B_{i}}{\alpha^{d-2+i}}+p\frac{B^{(1)}_{i}}{\alpha^{i-2}}L^{2}-\frac{C_{d}^{[1]}B_{i}}{\epsilon^{d-2}}\frac{L^{i}}{\epsilon^{i}}-pB^{(1)}_{i}\frac{L^{i}}{\epsilon^{i-2}}\Bigg)-d_{2}\alpha^{2}L^{2}\Bigg],\end{split}

where
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
B_{i}=A_{i}/(2-d-i)
,
B_{i}^{(1)}=A_{i}^{(1)}/(2-i)
.

10
Conclusions

We begin this section by summarising the main results of this paper. We have considered the properties of entanglement entropy on a generic static background (
4.9
), (
5.8
). This analysis requires the replica stress tensor, which is determined using the replica stress tensor conservation (
6.16
) as well as other defining properties, such as the trace equation in a CFT. Note that the geometry of the entangling region is captured by the metric on the replica space. On static backgrounds we can uniquely and explicitly solve for the replica stress tensor expectation value using the replica stress tensor conservation equations (
6.55
), (
6.64
), (
6.65
) and trace equation (
6.62
) in the case of a CFT. The
O(q-1)
replica stress tensor expectation value completely determines the scaling of the entanglement entropy (see (
5.51
)).

Most computations of entanglement and Rényi entropies have been limited to vacuum states on flat space in specific entangling subregions. The approach presented in this paper provides a way to calculate the scaling of Rényi and entanglement entropies (and the quantities themselves up to a constant) for general states and subregions on arbitrary backgrounds. It also sets up the methodology required to calculate replica stress tensor correlations in any state given the corresponding stress tensor correlations on the base manifold.

We have presented explicit computations for vacuum and quasithermal states for a subregion bounded by two planes in flat space using the replica metric corresponding to an unsquashed cone (
3.11
). We have also given explicit computations in spherical and cylindrical entangling boundaries in flat space. A natural extension would be to consider other states and subregions on static backgrounds. While our focus has been on specific scaling transformations, we could analyse in a similar way the behaviour of entanglement under generic variations, including deformations of the background metric, using (
4.9
).

Another straightforward extension would be to consider other states (non vacuum and non thermal) on flat backgrounds in higher dimensions. The replica conservation and trace equations that determine
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[1]}
will now have sources
\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle^{[0]}
and
\mathcal{A}^{[1]}[g_{\mu\nu}^{[q]}]
(in even dimensions). We could also consider a more complicated entangling boundary geometry. The expansion of the metric around a general foliation is given by (
3.26
) and the corresponding replica metric is given in (
3.28
). Using these in the replica conservation and trace equations gives the replica stress tensor expectation value, and using that one can obtain the scaling of entanglement entropy and also the Rényi entropy for any entangling boundary of interest.

Further analysis of entanglement and its variations in non-flat static backgrounds would use the replica metric
(
3.25
)
which has explicit dependence on the extrinsic curvature of the entangling boundary. In this generic context the replica conservation and trace equations are more complicated, due to the non-vanishing connection on the base space, although still straightforwardly solvable case by case.

To extend to stationary black holes, one would need to extend the computation of entanglement to the corresponding stationary backgrounds. One way of approaching this would be to make use of the generating boost or rotation transforms that relate stationary and static black holes. For example, the defining equations for the replica stress tensor in the stationary case will be related to those in the static case by the corresponding generating transformations. A more ambitious step would be to extend our analysis to entanglement in generic backgrounds, in which there is no time translation symmetry. Interesting first steps in this direction could follow from considering de Sitter type backgrounds.

Carrollian and celestial CFTs have recently been extensively discussed in the context of holography in asymptotically flat spacetimes. Since our computation is a field theory computation using replica trick and does not rely on holography or on the nature of the asymptotics of the space, one could straightforwardly extend our methodology to compute entanglement entropy in Carrollian and celestial field theories. The trace and conservation equations will be modified, reflecting the underlying symmetry structure of these theories
[
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
]
. It would be interesting to explore entanglement in such theories, and relate to the proposed holographic descriptions. More generally, explicit computations of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
in higher dimensions also facilitate exploration of the quantum focussing conjecture
[
78
]
, with the possibility of refining bounds further.

Let us now turn to the connections between our analysis and islands of entanglement.
The premise of islands follows from the semi-classical prescription for entropy, i.e. the quantum extremal surface approach.
The quantum extremal surface (QES) prescription
[
30
]
states that the generalised entropy associated with a subregion
A
is obtained from extremising the contributions of the geometric area and the entanglement entropy of the quantum fields in subregion
A
:

S_{\hbar^{0}}(A)=\textnormal{min}\Biggr[\underset{A}{\textnormal{ext}}\left(S_{\textnormal{gen}}=S_{\textnormal{QFT}}(A)+S_{\textnormal{area}}(\partial A)\right)\Biggr].

(10.1)

The quantum extremal surface is used in the island prescription
[
33
,
32
]
as a way to recover the Page curve and restore unitarity, by including quantum
O(\hbar^{0})
corrections to the Hawking radiation from an evaporating black hole.

Specifically, if
S[\textbf{Rad}]
is the total entropy and
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}[A]
is the renormalised entropy of the quantum fields (here radiation) associated with a subregion
A
, we have

S[\textbf{Rad}]=\textnormal{min}\Biggr[\underset{A}{\textnormal{ext}}\left(S_{\textnormal{gen}}=S_{\textnormal{QFT}}(A)+S_{\textnormal{area}}(\partial A)\right)\Biggr].

(10.2)

We vary the subregion
A
until the generalised entropy is minimized. This minimum generalised entropy associated with a particular subregion gives the entropy of the radiation in the quantum description (to first order in the
\hbar
expansion). It has been shown to recover the Page curve in certain setups.

If the subregion
A_{min}
is finite i.e., it does not share a boundary with asymptotic infinity, then the complementary region (which has the same entropy, provided we started with a pure state) will be disconnected and has a region whose boundaries are disconnected from the asymptotic infinity. The region which is disconnected is termed an island, and the presence of islands has been shown to be key in recovering the Page curve.

Now let us consider the extremisation of the generalised entropy in a static black hole background. Given the underlying symmetry of such a background, the extremisation corresponds to considering quantum fields in a slab region bounded by inner and outer radii, and varying the radius. This is precisely the variation that we have considered in this paper. The area term in the generalised entropy is purely geometric and typically behaves monotonically in the radius. The existence of islands, i.e. the quantum extremal surface having a component that is disconnected from asymptotic infinity, can thus be translated to conditions on the scaling of
S_{\rm QFT}
with the radius. This analysis will be the focus of our upcoming work.

It was shown in
[
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
, that in theories with long-range gravity where Gauss law holds, islands which by definition are disconnected from the asymptotic region of spacetime do not constitute consistent entanglement wedges. This is because the energy of an excitation localized to the island can be detected from outside the island. It was argued that this can be resolved if islands appear in conjunction with a massive graviton. Resolutions retaining massless graviton, have been discussed in the context of the de Sitter information paradox
[
83
]
and in the presence of black holes with baby universes
[
84
]
. It will be interesting to see how our conditions on the scaling of
S_{\textnormal{QFT}}
for the existence of islands, which in turn impose conditions on the QFT spectrum, compare with these.

Appendix B
State and (reduced) density matrix in a QFT

In this section, we will review the literature on how to construct states and density matrices in the path integral formalism
[
48
]
.

Path integrals and states:

Consider a generic set of fields denoted by
\phi_{i}(x)
. A Lorentzian Path integral defines a transition amplitude under unitary time evolution from a state
\rvert\phi_{1}(x)\rangle
to a state
\langle\phi_{2}(x)\rvert
.

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert e^{-i(t_{2}-t_{1})H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle=\int_{\phi(\vec{x},t_{1})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})}^{\phi(\vec{x},t_{2})=\phi_{2}(\vec{x})}D\phi e^{iS[\phi]}=\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert\psi\rangle.

(B.1)

Figure 10
:
Lorentzian Path integral

This can be interpreted as the overlap/ inner product of state
\rvert\psi\rangle=e^{-i(t_{2}-t_{1})H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
\rvert\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rangle
. The state so defined satisfies the Schrodinger equation by construction.

We can Wick rotate to the Euclidean time (
t\to t^{\prime}=-i\tau
), to get the Euclidean path integral

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert e^{-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle=\int_{\phi(\vec{x},\tau_{1})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})}^{\phi(\vec{x},\tau_{2})=\phi_{2}(\vec{x})}D\phi e^{-S_{E}[\phi]}=\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert\psi\rangle.

(B.2)

Figure 11
:
Euclidean Path integral

This gives a transition amplitude as well, but the imaginary time evolution is not unitary i.e.,
e^{-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})H}
is not a unitary operator. This can be viewed as an overlap of states. The transition amplitude can again be interpreted as the overlap with
\rvert\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rangle
of the state

\rvert\psi\rangle=e^{-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle.

(B.3)

If we now define a
cut
as a spatial slice (codimension-1 surface) of the Euclidean manifold, then one can formally think of a Euclidean path integral with one set of boundary conditions and one open cut (i.e., cut with unspecified boundary condition) as a quantum state i.e.,

\rvert\psi\rangle=e^{-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle=\int_{\phi(\vec{x},\tau_{1})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})}D\phi e^{-S_{E}[\phi]}.

(B.4)

Figure 12
:
Path integral with one open cut - state

The data on the top cut (
\tau=\tau_{2}
) is left unspecified. The functional
\rvert\psi\rangle
along with the top boundary condition given by
\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert
gives the complex numbers
\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert\psi\rangle
. States are defined on a spatial slice and do not care about Lorentzian vs Euclidean path integral. The state defined above by a Euclidean path integral is a state in the Hilbert space of the Lorentzian theory.

Density matrix using Path integrals:

A density matrix is an operator which takes a bra and a ket and produces a complex number. Thus any path integral with two identically shaped open cuts defines a density matrix (unnormalised).

Figure 13
:
An example of a path integral with two identical open cuts - density matrix

Consider the density matrix corresponding to the state defined by (
B.4
) i.e.,

\rho=\rvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvert.

(B.5)

Figure 14
:
Density matrix corresponding to a particular state

If
\phi_{2}(x)
and
\phi_{3}(x)
are the respective boundary conditions at the two open cuts, we have,

\langle\phi_{3}(\vec{x})\rvert\rho\rvert\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rangle=\langle\phi_{3}(\vec{x})\rvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvert\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rangle.

(B.6)

This can be computed using the path integral expressions given by (
B.2
).

The trace of the density matrix

Z=Tr\rho=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\langle\phi(\vec{x})\rvert\rho\rvert\phi(\vec{x})\rangle=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\langle\psi\rvert\phi(\vec{x})\rangle\langle\phi(\vec{x})\rvert\psi\rangle=\langle\psi\rvert\psi\rangle,

(B.7)

is the path integral with the two open cuts having the same boundary condition
\phi(\vec{x})
and summed over all possible field configurations
\phi(\vec{x})
i.e., gluing the two open cuts.

Example 1:

The vacuum/ ground state:
Let us start with a state
\rvert X\rangle
. Expanding in the energy eigenstate
\rvert n\rangle
, we have,

\rvert X\rangle=\sum_{n}x_{n}\rvert n\rangle;\quad H\rvert n\rangle=E_{n}\rvert n\rangle.

(B.8)

We can define the (unnormalised) ground state by doing a path integral that extends to infinity in one direction i.e., by evolving over a long Euclidean time

e^{-\tau H}\rvert X\rangle\approx e^{-\tau E_{0}}x_{0}\rvert 0\rangle;\quad\tau\to\infty.

(B.9)

Figure 15
:
Vacuum state

The trace of the vacuum density matrix

\displaystyle Z=Tr\rho

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\langle\phi(\vec{x})\rvert\rho_{vacuum}\rvert\phi(\vec{x})\rangle=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\langle\ 0\rvert\phi(\vec{x})\rangle\langle\phi(\vec{x})\rvert 0\rangle=\langle 0\rvert 0\rangle

\displaystyle=\int D\phi e^{-S_{E}[\phi]}.

(B.10)

Figure 16
:
Trace of the vacuum density matrix

The path integral on the lower half plane produces
\rvert 0\rangle
, the path integral on the upper half plane produces
\langle 0\rvert
, and summing over all possible boundary conditions
\phi(\vec{x})
in the middle at
\tau=0
, glues the half-planes together giving the trace.

Example 2:

The thermal density matrix: Consider

\rho_{thermal}=e^{-\beta H}.

(B.11)

We have,

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert\rho_{thermal}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\rvert e^{-\beta H}\rvert\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\phi(x,\tau_{1})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})}^{\phi(x,\tau_{2})=\phi_{2}(\vec{x})}D[\phi]e^{-S_{E}}.

(B.12)

The trace

\displaystyle Z(\beta)

\displaystyle=Tre^{-\beta H}=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\langle\phi(\vec{x})\rvert e^{-\beta H}\rvert\phi(\vec{x})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi(\vec{x})}\int_{\phi(x,\tau_{1})=\phi(\vec{x})}^{\phi(x,\tau_{2})=\phi(\vec{x})}D[\phi]e^{-S_{E}}=\int_{S_{1}^{\tau}}D[\phi]e^{-S_{E}},

(B.13)

\rho_{A}=Tr_{\bar{A}}\rho

\phi(\vec{x})

only along the complement region

has the following path integral field boundary conditions along the open cuts

\begin{split}&\phi(\text{cut 1})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\text{ $\forall$ x },\\
&\phi(\text{cut 2})=\phi_{2}(\vec{x})\text{ if $x\in A$ },\\
&\phi(\text{cut 2})=\phi_{1}(\vec{x})\text{ if $x\notin A$ },\end{split}
